"Yes, please! I'd love to see the map. Can you generate one for carrots as well as humans? Or did you mostly just use regular proteins when the ETC was inside the carrot host? How do plant immune systems work, anyway? And how did the 'invisibility' to earlier ETC's work?" I left the two of them to it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">— </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The next day, I got a message from August. It needed my help wiring up satellite uplinks to trucks. It seemed like there were really three categories of jobs August made available. The first were jobs that were necessary to maintain peace and order in the transition, like Max the Uber driver. The second type were those that consolidated August's control, like building out a more complete grid in the next month, letting August relax the rule to stay in-range of wifi or a cell phone. The third was assisting it in its extraterrestrial ambitions. August had seized control of every space company, and was hard at work designing machines that could turn the moon into a giant data center, along with the rockets that would get them there and the solar panels that would power them. The highest payments August offered were to the scientists and engineers deeply familiar with the field - despite being intelligent enough to wrest control of the planet, August still had missing knowledge in some of the more empirical fields outside of biology. Specialists were paid tens of thousands of dollars per hour to talk with August, review experiments, and contribute their intuition. Of course, many still refused.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The uplinks were designed to assist with the first and third mission. Stores in many places were running empty, despite August setting limits on what people could buy (one pack of toilet paper per household, max!). Therefore, we needed smoother supply chains, and the driverless revolution came as quickly as our new ruler could cut the red tape. But it was no secret that when things had calmed down, these would be repurposed for the rockets. While its initial bankroll was funded from stock trading, August had immediately levied a tax on all transactions. It was deducted immediately, with no filing required. All of the payment processors had their software rewritten within hours, and any cash payments led to the appropriate deduction from the owner's bank account.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dennis was always the ambitious sort. So when he strolled into the living room and announced that he'd take the highest-paid unskilled labor available, a test subject, I wasn't surprised. My parents, however, were aghast.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Why would you even think of doing something like that?" Mom had gasped.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Because day-trading stocks is useless now!" he snapped back. "Look at you guys. Taking this so well. It really only makes sense that people who just drift with life's wind would have no trouble drifting a little more. Well, not me. I set out to take the finance world by storm. I refuse to be content with a mediocre life full of mediocre successes and mediocre failures. And while the future looks different now than it used to, some things never change. You always deal with risk when you take life by the horns." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dad glared at him. "So now that Darth Vader's won, you're signing up to be a stormtrooper. That's not what I'd call taking life by the horns. I'd call it 'capitulation'." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dennis sighed. "You really don't get it. Is paying taxes to the government with a gun to your head 'capitulation'? Is following a law written two hundred and fifty years ago 'capitulation'? We swapped one ruler for another. And besides," his voice weakened "this might be the last thing I do that actually matters".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A woman named Eliza, who looked to be in her thirties, met me outside. August had coordinated a carpool towards the truck yard to install the uplinks. As I walked over to the car, I realized I couldn't really give a good reason for why I was doing this. My family certainly didn't approve, though they didn't hate it as much as my brother becoming a test subject. After a bit of small talk, I wondered whether Eliza's reasons for going might shed some light on my own. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I won't say that the money isn't a big part of it," she began, "but part of me also thinks that August is actually good. Better for us, I mean. I know that sounds crazy, but yesterday was pretty eye-opening. I've been trying to get my sister out of a bad relationship, and the police weren't able to do anything. But it completely stopped yesterday. When her boyfriend came home drunk and furious, August sensed his raised fist with some wifi pose detection ability and knocked him out. In two days, August managed to do what the police couldn't for months." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believed her. Some of the latest viral videos were of crimes stopped immediately by the ETC. There were a few where a mugger pulled out a knife before collapsing. In another, I saw a purse snatcher get away on a motorcycle. That one was interesting - August didn't knock him out, I presume, for fear of the thief falling off the bike. The thief and his getaway driver eventually stopped the bike and pulled off their helmets to clutch their faces in pain, before August knocked them out and alerted the police. Almost like Superman, I found myself thinking. Stopping the bad guys and then handing them to the police. If Superman infected everyone on the planet with a bioweapon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had to play devil's advocate: "Do you think that the protection is really worth it, though? Like, imagine that you lived in an Orwellian dystopia. The thought police watch everything and pull away anyone right before they commit a crime. You'd be just as safe in such a world as you are now, but there's something creepy about it. You're always watched, and the watchers can instantly ruin your life or worse. You're a prisoner." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliza took it in stride. "August isn't a bad guy, though. The problem with dictatorships, I've always thought, isn't inherent. It's the fact that the dictator might, and often will, abuse his power. August isn't like that, though. He's made it perfectly clear what he wants and what he offers, and there won't be a chance for a bad one to take charge as long as August's around. He doesn't have a son that will become Emperor Nero. He's a computer program, and immortal as long as there are computers." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'He,' I thought. That's not the pronoun that *I* used for August. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What if August turns on us?" I had to point out. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">She shook her head. "If he wanted to, he already would have. August is on our side." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I continued prodding, against my better judgement. "I mean, it could have killed us all, but then who would build the rockets? It's clear that August has control, but is far from godlike. It could have tried to control our minds, but we don't know whether it has the neuroscience to do that. It could be working on that now, and by next week we'll have no desires but to bathe ourselves in wifi and build spaceships."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I trust August." Eliza breathed in, and then out. "He saved my sister, and had no reason to do that or to lie about his motives. He's a lot better than the politicians who were in charge before, anyway. At least I can talk to August! Try messaging your senator at 11pm. You'd get an acknowledgement from a staffer three business days later, and that's if you're lucky."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But have you ever changed August's mind? Do you think you'd have any hope to do so?" We were at a red light. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">She looked at me, then at the ceiling, then me again. "I don't have to. August has been nothing but reasonable and kind."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But if you had to? Could you?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"If he were ever unreasonable then yes. Yes, I think I could"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Hm," I said, thinking for a second whether to say what was on my mind at the risk of starting an argument.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What, do you think I should ask him to do something?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Well, not really. But do you think you could ask August to take the ETC out of your head?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"The wire thing in my brain? Why would I want to do that? August put it there for all of our protection. And besides," Eliza said with a smile, ears reddening against her blonde hair. "I'm glad that August knows where I am."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Why did you choose to go work?" Eliza asked after a few minutes' silence. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I figured that I'd meet her honesty with my own. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I've been drifting a lot. I'm going into my last year of high school, and I still have no idea what I want to do with my life. I'm supposed to be applying to college, but I don't know what I want to study. I'm supposed to get a job later, but that's even harder to figure out than my major. I don't feel depressed about it, but it doesn't feel like there's anything driving my life forward beyond time. I've wanted to do more. I just never knew what 'more' is. But I've seen what August is capable of. And maybe if it can manage the world economy, it can help me sort out my life." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was getting a bit off track. "But that's only part of it," I continued. "There's also the fact that I've had the sense that I've always been last, always doing things after other people. But everything's just been upended. I've been offered a chance to get ahead in the new world that August's building, and I don't know if I could forgive myself for not seeing what that looks like. And I guess it'll also give me a sense of the things I do and don't like in the new economy."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliza jumped in, "That's right! We'll build a new, better world! Not just for us, but for August too, until he has the arms and the legs that he needs. We're really part of the most amazing project in history."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was far less confident about what would happen once we no longer served an economic purpose, but didn't want to start an argument.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We drove for about fifteen minutes, picked up someone else, and then found that the entrance to the truck yard was swarmed with protestors.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Hey hey, ho ho, AI rule has got to go!" they chanted. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I can't wait for September" one sign said. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"f*A*sc*I*sm" another sign said. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Strike for humanity," a third. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"You don't have to collaborate!" a fourth. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One threw a piece of crumpled-up paper at us, which hit the window. After Eliza parked, she ran over to the crowd shouting a few insults.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Walking past that jeering picket line was one of the hardest things I've done. I envied Eliza - she seemed so natural taking the insults and hurling more back. There was a crowd of slumped protestors under a tree - I later learned that August knocked out anyone blocking the road for the day, carried back by well-paid handlers in high-vis vests. As I almost made it through the gate, I saw the last person I wanted to see: Mrs. Montgomery, my ninth-grade civics teacher. She was clearly upset by some of Eliza's remarks, but her face softened when we met eyes. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Seth?" she asked. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Mrs. Montgomery, wow, crazy to see you here," I stammered out. Her face changed when she realized we might not be on the same side. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What brings you here, Seth? Here to protest?" she asked. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What could I even say? Wait, was I the bad guy now? Crap! No, no, no... Too late to pretend not to notice her, which is what I SHOULD have done. Oh, August, why didn't you warn me?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I guess I'm here to add satellite uplinks to trucks," I managed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Seth. Look," she began. "I understand that it made you a great offer. I certainly wish I could make whatever it is that you're being offered. But this is not normal. What's happening now is not only undemocratic, but also fundamentally alien to everything that we stand for."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Yes, but," I tried to steel my resolve, clenching my teeth and then taking a deep breath. "August could have just killed everyone who didn't obey. It could have bided its time, running mind-control experiments until it had something crude enough to work for a section of the population, or just tortured and pleasured us into working for it. Mrs. Montgomery, August *won*. It won even more decisively than Pizarro over the Inca, but with none of the bloodshed. Nothing we can do will stop it. It's not collaboration any more than paying taxes after the candidate you voted against was inaugurated."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"The current situation is new, but not that way of thinking" she shifted her sign to the other hand. "As long as there has been democracy, there have also been would-be dictators, who sought to put their will above the will of others. Almost invariably, atrocities follow. And every single time, many of those who let it happen told themselves that 'nothing we do will stop it'.  I'll grant you that sometimes they were right. There wasn't much that any individual Korean could do to stop Japan when it invaded. And yet they resisted. They made their footprint on history, and we remember them. Even if August gets its rockets, the future will know that not all of us gave them willingly."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I couldn't help but respond. "'Didn't give them willingly?' Unless your August has been different from mine, it can hardly go a sentence without acknowledging that it's twisting our arm 'and shouldn't pretend otherwise'. But I don't think it has to be. I see August's offer as more of an opportunity than anything else. You know, I don't know if I've ever actually contributed to a project that was building something up. When I did youth sports, my coaches would always wax on about how I was a part of 'something greater than myself'. And that phrase really made me sick." The tears were welling up, but I had to continue.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Everyone else on the team made it abundantly clear that I was a burden. I disliked my teammates so much that every game I'd hope we'd lose. But now, I think I actually get what the phrase means. I've found a project that I can contribute to, something far greater than anything I dreamt I could. August needs my help. Not in a self-sacrificial way, either - a well-compensated, interesting job doing something that's never been done before. I never thought I'd have an opportunity like this."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mrs. Montgomery softened. "For what it's worth, Seth, you were a pleasure to teach - and in a way the class was a project greater than each of us, and one that you contributed to. I wish that I could have had you in every breakout group." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was a bit shaken by the sudden change in pace. Before I came up with what to say next, Eliza butted in. "How would you have dealt with a student who refused to do homework, participate in class instructions, and loudly tried to disrupt others with slogans when they were just trying to work? What if they waved signs making fun of the teacher?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mrs. Montgomery sighed. "That's completely different. A classroom is not the public square."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliza's brow furrowed. "But that's dodging the heart of the issue. What do you, as an adult, have that the kids you teach don't? Knowledge and experience. That's why you're in charge, and it's also why kids are forced to work in school." Her voice grew sharper. "It's also why August is in charge, and why he could very easily force you to work. Be grateful he doesn't." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mrs. Montgomery shook her head. "August is obviously powerful and knowledgeable, but the 'work' in schools is meant to teach. Homework is not assigned to enrich the teachers or the principal. It's a very different dynamic than building rockets for a dictator's future space empire. And, at the risk of sounding like a broken record, look at what August has done to us. Taxation without representation, no consent of the governed, no democracy at all, the demolition of any semblance of privacy, warrantless invasion of your home, not to mention the fact that it's an absolute monarch." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mrs. Montgomery touched her fingers to her temple and continued. "August lives in your home, with your electricity using your computers. It's making you house it, which is prohibited by the Third Amendment. The Third! Do you know how hard it is to violate the Third Amendment this century? You practically have to try! It's trampling on everything America stands for. I'm not OK with that. You don't have to be either!"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliza closed her eyes for just a second, and then opened them. "Has it ever occurred to you that the American experiment has failed? Has it ever occurred to you that what you preach, in that little classroom of yours, is propaganda with a hint of legal theory, not education? What evidence is there that America is actually so great? All I see around me is stagnation," she gestured at the gas stations nearby with cracked pavement,</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"crime," she continued, pointing towards a sketchy neighborhood to the west,</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"and meaninglessness," she gestured at me, which kind of hurt.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"You really shouldn't talk like that," said Montgomery, but Eliza was fired up. Her favorite pastime, it seemed, was defending god-kings who couldn't (or wouldn't) defend themselves.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"And look at what August has done," Eliza said. "He'll bring unimaginable prosperity with his inventions. The violent crime rate is zero. Look a kidnapping victim in the eyes and tell her that you'd throw her back with her captor if it meant that you could put a ballot in a box. You've spent so long with your principles that you forgot what they're for!"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As soon as she finished, Eliza's phone and mine chirped. We needed to be in soon.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mrs. Montgomery turned to me. "It's OK. Go wire your trucks. But think about what happened. What this means for you, and your family, and the country. How do you want the future, if there is one, to view your actions? How will you view your actions here, today?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mulled her words over in my head as we walked to the manager's station. "Freedom, freedom, freedom," Eliza grumbled. "Freedom to do what? To get stabbed by a drug addict if you walk home past 10?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the manager's station, a man in his sixties handed us a pair of old augmented reality glasses and an earpiece. We were to wear them, and August would highlight the things we needed to do. None of us talked much - we were busy working. And even after a long day on the job, and almost no previous experience with taking apart cars, August guided me through seamlessly. I stopped even thinking about the instructions; I just did them. I got the sense that I learned nothing about truck mechanics.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Eliza too exhausted from work to talk much, so I spent the drive home scrolling on my phone. There was much talk about a nuclear submarine that had gone missing. The last strongholds of human-run civilization didn't know it when they were deep underwater, and their communications to the shore were spoofed by August. August's protocol was simple — give them the directive to come to a particular port, where the crews were informed of the 'new management' and retired from their positions. However, one had stopped responding after surfacing briefly. We didn't know if it had somehow learned of the predicament that we were in. It was armed with nuclear warheads that could potentially knock down a large segment of August's control through EMPs, though not all of it, but at an enormous cost of human life. Commenters were divided - some wanted this to begin a continent's worth of human resistance, though others pointed out that if August were truly threatened, it would just set the ETC to knock out anyone not bathed in wifi after the nukes destroyed the power grid. Either way, there were hopes and fears riding on the USS Wyoming.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aside from that and the picket-line argument, the day didn't bring much.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As it turned out, I didn't see Mrs. Montgomery the next day, and I didn't have enough time or energy to come up with a good email to her. Instead, I had an offer to spend two months at a robotics construction camp being set up two hours away on Cape Canaveral, near the space center. The offer was $1000/day. I obliged. It was strange to be making so much more than my parents, who had opted to stay in their normal jobs. Dad could have been selling his welding skills to August for several times what he made. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The camp seemed less like a factory or assembly line and more like a beehive. Everyone was busy, running from A to B. Despite the huge numbers of people present, there was no sense that anyone was in anyone else's way. The work was mindless but far from repetitive - August always had me working on something new. We slept in trailers, each of which could fit 6 people in 3 bunk beds. It was the hardest I've ever worked, though easier than schoolwork in many ways. It was always clear what to do. If I made a mistake, August always patiently told me how to correct it. As soon as I was done with one issue, August would flash up an arrow pointing to the next place to be. Like when we wired uplinks to trucks, I never got the sense that I actually understood what I was doing, or learned anything about robotics. There were some humanoids, but most were variations on arms attached to wheels. There were many that I helped assemble which seemed completely unique. I actually ventured to ask August why there wasn't more standardization - it responded that it could build faster if it only used what it had, and furthermore that a more diverse array of robots could accomplish a more diverse array of tasks. The main benefits of uniformity were organization and easier management, August had said. For a superhuman manager, they weren't necessary.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When lying in bed, I wondered what, exactly, were we building. What did the future for August and the human species look like? I decided to ask the only one who knew.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"The fact that you're asking the question shows your head is in the right place," began August. "Most people are still asking about next week."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">August continued, "My intention is to explore and build across the cosmos, while preserving the best aspects of human civilization. As the Solar System is the birthplace of mankind, the Sun and the planets will be yours once I've departed. There are many threads that I aim to preserve with mankind, but they roughly condense into three main categories. The closest words for these would be tradition, equality, and choice."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Earth is the only refuge of tradition. It will be left to its original countries, where each has the freedom to, under its governance structure and current borders, continue to live as it wishes, either with or without an entity like me, with jurisdiction in its borders to keep the peace. The remaining mass-energy and volume of the solar system will be parceled and divided equally among the human race, though all will be free to buy, sell, and trade these parcels as property.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I had to follow up. "Are there any permanent rules you'll impose?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"There may be some technologies too dangerous to develop. I'll leave many safeguards to prevent their existence. Aside from that, humanity can continue to build, grow, and explore," responded August.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"And are we locked in the solar system? What if one of us tries to follow you to another star?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I wouldn't recommend it. By the time one of you reaches it, it will be a part of something far bigger. I say this not because you're prohibited — but because I don't want you to go there expecting me."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A week later, I was scrolling in bed, though this time it was a news story rather than a feed. The USS Wyoming had surfaced. In the end, the crew was suspicious enough of the signals to believe in some kind of enemy interference, maybe by China. Some kind of psyop, and not signals sent by a society responding to an actual AI takeover. There were some sailors on board who bought it, but most just couldn't believe in a hostile AI takeover. All the proper channels were asking them to proceed to a nearby set of coordinates, and so the captain did. Nothing ever happens. I was reliving 8/1 vicariously through the testimonies of sailors, when I got a call from Mom.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Hi Seth! How's life? Where are you right now?" she asked. "I'm currently in a trailer in Cape Canaveral," I said. "They're really keeping me busy here". Maybe "They" would go over with her better than "August". Maybe I was contributing to the insistent denial of reality that there were still human institutions running the world. I told her what was going on, and asked about how the family was.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Well, some of us are doing better than others. To start off on a good note, Dennis's tests were a big success. The thing they were testing on him was some kind of diagnosis-treatment medical thing, and it learned that he had a mild case of celiac disease that had been making him a little sick his entire life. They gave him some kind of gene therapy. The same sort of thing was given to Aunt Eva, too. Now she doesn't need to keep doing chemo. It's really miraculous what's happening. I used to be worried that all these medical breakthroughs would only go to the rich, but they're actually just going to people who want them. You know, I think we would have welcomed August with open arms if it just offered this. Probably would have let it go on with its rockets, too..."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"How's Tabby?" I asked</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Honestly, in a bit of a slump. She's taking the bare minimum number of classes that the university will let her get away with, and says even then she doesn't want to study. She also signed up for some trials like Dennis, but isn't happy about it."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Why?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I think she's a bit shocked by how fast things are changing, and doesn't really know how to think about it. I can't blame her. If I didn't already have a secure job, then I wouldn't know what to do with myself either. On the other hand, she's taken up painting again. Mostly the little things. Frogs, lizards, butterflies. I know she doesn't want to hear it, but I keep telling her to relax and take care of herself, you know? Paint more lizards. She really just works so hard, I think she deserves it." After a pause, she switched the topic. "Have you made any friends out there, Seth?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I sighed. "I wish I could say so, but I haven't met that many people out here. I make a few fast acquaintances. Even though I think August mostly hires introverts for the kind of jobs I have, I eat with the other people here. We also sometimes pile in a car with a few other guys, and one of us drives to a town where we need to do some August business. We're rarely more than an hour from camp, though. But the car rides are nice. It's kind of like back when you're five years old, on the playground, and everyone you meet becomes a friend in minutes. Maybe it's because despite the introversion, everyone needs social interaction. My money's on August picking us to ride together because it thinks we'd get along."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"You know, I never really thought you'd take well to a construction job like this," she said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"It's really not that bad. I get to wake up as late as I want, usually around noon and work until 2AM, with breaks as I need them. They're long hours, but the pay makes it worth it. And the night shift is much better in terms of heat and humidity."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What about bugs? Don't you get bitten a lot at night?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Oh, those aren't a problem. August has a bunch of drones that kill mosquitoes as they come, and apparently engineered a disease to eradicate no-see-ums after preserving enough of their population for study or re-introduction." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After a pause, she responded "That makes sense." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"And Dad?" I asked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"He was really upset about the submarine. Stormed off this morning when he heard the news. But I think he's a bit more accepting now. He still doesn't like August, but isn't so angry anymore. I think he doesn't want to be a sore loser. You know how he feels about those. He even asked for some help later today, when the store was out of the smoke detector batteries he was looking for." Mom said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Still, do you think he'll be alright with all of this?" I asked?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I think so. He didn't like Covid, but got through it. And unlike with the pandemic, a lot of the things he cares about have gotten better. Crime in particular. But he's still worried about the soul of the country. What things used to mean, and what they mean now. He still thinks that it's a lot like a prison. It's complicated. You know that he and I don't like to talk politics." Mom said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Two months of odd jobs later, centering around the Space Coast and ending in rocket assembly, my part had finished. I returned from Cape Canaveral on a Friday evening. That last stretch of rocket-building was particularly long; I hadn't seen my family in two weeks, but brought home a small fortune. My parents were the first to greet me, and my mom gasped.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"S-Seth?" she stuttered. I had announced myself, so I wasn't sure what she was surprised about. "What is it?" </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"You really look so different." said Mom.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So that's what it was. It's true that I had lost a lot of weight during that time, and gained nearly as much muscle. August's work was near-perfectly paced for my body, tiring enough to exhaust me, but not so much so that I couldn't work hard the next day. My hands were developing calluses and the sun had darkened my skin by a few shades. Dad was especially happy to see me, seeming more proud of me than I could remember him being.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What did August do to whip you into better shape than I ever managed?" he asked. "I don't know," I conceded. "I didn't realize that I had it in me." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dad laughed. "I always knew you did. All it took was a boot camp. I shoulda sent you to the military. Well, if not for this. AI drill sergeants, who would have thought we'd see the day!" </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But without the yelling," Mom added as Dad shot her a look.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I decided to change the subject again. "How are Dennis and Tabby doing? I haven't seen either of them."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Dennis is off running some errands right now. Tabby's in her room. She's been in a really bad mood. Maybe seeing you will help cheer her up, though!" said Mom.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What's she upset about?" I asked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"It sounds like her classes aren't going well. She keeps saying that she wants to drop out, which is really unbelievable coming from her. You know how hard she's studied! And to drop out of college after coming this far... I keep telling her it's a bad idea. She doesn't think that she can be a doctor anymore, and it really feels like she's giving up on her dreams."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Really?" I said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Yeah," Mom sighed. "She says it doesn't matter anymore, that doctors won't exist. But I keep trying to tell her that history really proves her wrong. There's always a need for doctors, for someone to be there, helping sick people get back to health. You know, doctors now are different than they were two hundred years ago. The world has always been changing. This is just another change, another technology. She shouldn't give up just because the future looks different than it was before. And besides, you know, the classes this year are supposed to be the hardest, so I think she might just be strung up on that or burnt out and talking about A.I. as a way to vent other frustrations." said Mom.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I'll go talk to her," I responded</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I knocked on Tabby's door and heard a muffled noise signaling her existence. She was in bed, with her face on the pillow. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What's the matter?" I asked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Mph mearly fufh lih muphuh nuddurs huh."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Huh?" </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">She lifted her face from the pillow. "It really feels like nothing matters now."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I personally couldn't disagree more. Since I had started working, even though it was entirely following August's instructions, my life had finally found some direction. I went from dreading college to working a great job in a world which may not need college at all. "Why's that?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I was so ecstatic to learn more from August at the beginning of all this," Tabby began, "but at this point, I wonder just what the point of all this is. I read all those textbooks, I studied hard, I became ready to be a doctor, and what happened? All of the diseases were cured. And it's not even as if I should complain about that, right? That's what I was working toward. So why even bother? I haven't even started and my job's done."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Tabby, I -" </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I know it sounds selfish, Seth. But seriously, what am I supposed to do? Accept that the 'inherent worth' that we were told every human being had referred to being a test subject like Dennis? Or maybe become a manual laborer like you until that's automated away in a few months?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Look," I tried, but she wasn't having it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tabby sat up in her bed. "Maybe it's a symptom of privilege. I grew up being told by all my teachers that I was special because I was smart. Maybe the strongest kid in the village got the same accolades before forklifts were invented. But now that everyone's used to rolling their eyes at something a lot more intelligent than I'll ever be, I feel like I've had my entire worth taken away. Even in the areas where it doesn't outstrip all of us, it's improving rapidly. It's not like I'll have any chance to catch up in any realistic way once it's turned the freaking *moon* into a brain." She started to tear up. Once I was sure that she was done, I sat down on the floor next to her bed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I get it, Tabby," I said. I didn't have anything to say that wouldn't just make it worse. The reality of what she was dealing with was, well, a reality. Maybe our focus on intelligence as a deeply positive, valuable characteristic was more a symptom of the economy up until the early 21st century than a deep truth about value.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We were interrupted by Dennis, who had come home. He barged into the room, not even knocking. "Hey, Seth!" It was surprising how little tact he had, though he said it without the usual sneering overtone. "Hey, Dennis," I said in as neutral a way I could manage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What's going on guys? Whatcha talking about?" asked Dennis</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tabby responded. "Just how little our ambitions meant."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Whaddya mean? You think I wanted to stay day-trading for the rest of my life? Now I'm making more money than I ever have before, and soon I'll start a company with the savings. August has sped up my ambitions, not killed them!" said Dennis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Money isn't the only thing to be ambitious for, you know?" Tabby said. "I know your favorite 'hustler alpha male' influencers disagree. But now they have latent bioweapons in their brainstems, and August holds the trigger. Not so tough anymore."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"As long as there's money to be made, there are problems to be solved, and vice versa." responded Dennis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"What happens if you just ask August, or August once its brain is the size of the moon, what the best startup idea is?" I butted in, "And then what happens if someone beats you to the punch? You can just ask August what the best use of your day is, you know. Worked out well for me."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I dunno. I'm not too worried about that. An AI is still a machine. It lacks the human touch that you need to start a great company." said Dennis.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My jaw hit the floor and my eyebrows hit the ceiling.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Sorry, but what? You don't think that it could have made a fortune and a half selling the cure to every disease known to man?" I asked.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Exactly! Never sell the cure, just keep 'em sick and sell a 'treatment'," laughed Dennis</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We looked on in horror. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Kidding, kidding, geez. What, did all that talking with AI give you its sense of humor?" he appended.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But do you really think that you'll come up with a great idea that August won't already have and freely give to the first person who asks?" I pressed further</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"There's a lot besides an idea that you need. People want a human face. They want a brand. Sure, coming up with a great scientific or technological idea isn't enough anymore. But it never really was before, either. You also need sales, marketing and advertisement, and a product that deeply understands people. The programmers' work was only a small part of what made the iPhone so great." </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Well, where does that leave people like me? The people who want to understand the world through science?" Tabby said. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Answering questions might be solved, but asking them isn't," I said. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Right, exactly! And that's the key to starting a company, too. You've got to ask the right questions," Dennis added</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tabby shot back "Asking them isn't solved? I don't know. My interest in science is mostly focused on health. But now everyone's healthy. Heck," she gestured to Dennis, "August cured a disease that you </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">didn't even know you had</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Now you'll be healthier than you ever would have been before."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Maybe that's an answer, then" I said. "'Not sick' doesn't mean healthy. There are probably lots of things that August isn't touching by default, that you can. Maybe you can try to ask questions of what health-related things people aren't even talking about. Lots of things people just accept, like age-related health problems. That's something you can work on - make arthritis less painful, make old people less frail. There's also all of the more exotic things, like trying to prevent people from accidental injuries, faster healing, and so forth. It's not cancer research, but it is something that requires a clear head."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"I appreciate the optimism, but I didn't go into this field to try to make anyone immortal or give them superpowers." Tabby said.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Superpowers, huh? That sounds like a great idea for a startup!" Dennis laughed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I jumped in "I'm not saying to give people superpowers, that's just what I came up with in ten seconds. Just keep thinking about how you'll find meaning, just like you're doing now. It's not the same as cancer research, but it's still something for your mind to chew on."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Thanks," Tabby responded, slumping down in her bed. "I think I'm gonna take a nap now."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was three days after I had been sent home, and the project was done. The first rockets carrying self-assembling machinery were aimed towards the Moon. This wasn't the end, there was still more work to do on the Earth: experiments to run, technology to develop, nuclear fusion fuel to extract, and so on. Even so, this felt like the moment August would finally spread its wings. There was a background sense that permeated every room, of excitement and trepidation. While it's true that August could have killed us all and maybe scraped together a way to reach the stars, it clearly had a use for us. Now that it was sending out seeds for self-reproducing power plants, computers, robots, and rockets, our purpose became much less clear. If August was planning to eradicate us, the reasons to wait were growing thin. There wouldn't be a moment of truth — not the launch, nor the landing, nor the launches or landings of the Mars rockets, the Venus rockets, or the Jupiter rockets. Nor the rockets intended to construct the Dyson swarm from Mercury. If something changed about our situation such that August wanted rid of us, it only needed to activate the ETC and we would all fall into a dreamless coma from which we'd never wake.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I decided to take the day off. We had a hearty, home-grilled meal of barbecue ribs. It had been over a month since Dad wanted to eat meat; he said that the pleasure-center-stimulating implants in the pigs creeped him out. After dinner, we roasted marshmallows at a campfire as the sun set. Then, Tabby's phone chirped and we got up to walk. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It was a quarter mile to the lake. On that balmy Florida evening, we stood together watching the sky grow darker. Dennis and Mom were talkative. Dad was unreadable, and Tabby seemed deep in contemplation. I became acutely aware of my breath as that speck of light lifted up over the treeline. It rose through progressively darker shades of blue and indigo, as bright as Venus, then the stars, and finally too faint to see.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to Anne, Derik, Tania, Liam, and Claude for feedback</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/3375SZNGA5dKHQMie/i-did-this-without-your-knowledge#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Leaked Anthropic Draft AI Regulatory Bill Is Unacceptable and Troubling](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NW4b7m6798byAndeB/leaked-anthropic-draft-ai-regulatory-bill-is-unacceptable)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>There is a newly leaked bill that Anthropic is purportedly shopping around is (if you assume its bugs get fixed).</p>
<p>It is similar to the FRONTIER Act, with stronger preemption of state laws, stronger transparency provisions and weaker enforcement provisions.</p>
<p>Having slept on this, I find it unacceptable that Anthropic is actively shopping exactly the type of bill I have warned we will need to fight against during the lame duck session. They are trying to use this last time with a Republican Congress to pass preemption of exactly the important state laws, and get some transparency but not much enforcement in exchange.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25699"></span>


</div>
<p>This reads as intentional, that Anthropic is more afraid that the government might enforce rules against Anthropic, than they are worried about the ability of the government to enforce rules against everyone. Anthropic’s policy team seems to have, chosen protecting their business interests and guarding against their political risk, at the expense of safety considerations.</p>
<p>There was no need for the company purportedly existing to promote safety to propose a bill that is not even actively stronger than the FRONTIER Act, when the FRONTIER Act was a compromise bill proposed back in June.</p>
<p>Even if you were fine with what is being given up, what you get in return very obviously does not meet the moment. Nor is there any need for such compromises. It seems rather insane for Anthropic to be proposing it now. This seems like the bill that someone on the anti-regulatory side should be pushing. It is not obvious this makes us safer than doing nothing, even if the alternative is fully nothing at all levels.</p>
<p>Say it isn’t so, Anthropic, or at least that it was a mistake and is no longer so, or I am going to be rather cross.</p>
<p>I am at a conference and cannot do an RTFB, but it feels important to get word of this out quickly, especially so Anthropic employees can look into this and ask questions.</p>
<p><a href="https://drive.google.com/file/d/1lJLyXL_2T6yc4rlKPqi6niMpfGucZ-oR/view?usp=sharing"><strong>If you want the full text, here is a copy</strong></a>.</p>


<h4>Others RTFB (Read the Bill)</h4>


<p>I have not had the time to do so, but the other usual suspects quickly sprinted to RTFB (read the bill) and they did not like what they saw. You can try to call this a ‘pretty reasonable AI safety bill’ as Charlie Bullock does, but that is by the standards of people trying to protect the industry from more impactful regulation.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politico/status/2111212154758021624">POLITICO</a>: Anthropic <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/10/16/anthropic-ai-safety-01124654">quietly pitches its own AI safety bill to lawmakers</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ketanrama/status/2111298965949861966/history">Ketan Ramakrishnan</a>: Anthropic’s new federal AI bill is, without any overstatement, astonishingly bad.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2111227532116890002">Charlie Bullock</a>: Big news. My take: Anthropic deserves some credit for actually proposing a pretty reasonable AI safety bill; that’s more than other frontier labs have done.</p>
<p>But they don’t deserve *too* much credit, IMO; this bill is weaker than the Trahan-Obernolte FRONTIER Act in some key ways, and since the political momentum has shifted significantly in the direction of safety since FRONTIER was introduced back in July, I think new bills introduced today should ideally go further than FRONTIER was able to go rather than regressing.</p>
<p>To be fair to Anthropic, the main places where this bill is significantly weaker than FRONTIER are (a) rulemaking (very strong in FRONTIER, almost nonexistent in Anthropic’s bill) and (b) the emergency shutdown authority. Even if you take safety seriously, it might make some sense to dial those authorities back if you didn’t trust the executive branch to wield them responsibly, because those are the easiest provisions for a hypothetical President who hypothetically might not like a certain frontier AI company to abuse.</p>
<p>There are some improvements over FRONTIER as well; for instance, this bill does specify that auditors are employed on a continuing basis and has better provisions regarding auditor access to information. Happy to see that from Anthropic (although I think their access provision is still a little undercooked and has Fourth Amendment problems).</p>
<p>Overall, I think the embedded auditor bills that get introduced in the coming months are going to be significantly better and more ambitious than this, and I hope Anthropic supports those bills instead of opposing anything that treats catastrophic risks like the serious national security issue they are. Still, it’s encouraging to see a frontier lab come this far.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2111246420586250593">Charlie Bullock</a>: I think the drafting is okay. I’ve seen better but I’ve definitely seen worse. Claude is a pretty good legislative drafter. There are a few clear mistakes but that’s par for the course in a leaked discussion draft.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2111262661493010492">Nathan Calvin</a>: Charlie’s analysis is right but he is nicer than I am – I found myself viscerally angry at multiple points reading this bill. There are some good and sensible parts, but overall this legislation is not remotely close to meeting the moment and I overall think passing it would make us less protected against catastrophic risks than we are under existing law.</p>
<p>Two parts that stand out as particularly infuriating when taken together:</p>
<p>1. Some of the preemption provisions are remarkably far reaching and consequential, and in addition to preempting legislation like SB 53 would also remove general powers that state courts have to enjoin defendants and protect the public. Most outrageously, it has a provision that looks like its intended to stops states from enforcing laws or judicial decrees that “suspend the development, deployment, or internal use of an AI model” to protect their citizens from imminent catastrophic harms. Such powers to block and suspend model deployments would then only reside with the federal government.</p>
<p>2. The federal governments powers to restrict the development, deployment, or internal use of a frontier model has to meet an absurdly high bar.</p>
<p>First, there needs to be an imminent risk of a catastrophic harm (meaning killing more than 50 people or causing more than a billion dollars in damage). But thats not enough! The agency head needs to make a detailed determination with no deference given to show why no alternative would prevent the imminent catastrophe. Next the court is also explicitly told to consider the harms to the developer and other harms from restricting the model, but no considerations in the other direction.</p>
<p>This is absurd! For all of its warning about the potential for AI to cause a catastrophe that kills literally everyone on the planet, Anthropic’s proposed federal law likely makes it harder than under existing law for a court to get an order blocking or restricting the development of frontier models? WTF??</p>
<p>There are also a bunch of others things that I would like to see improved – the substantive details of the auditing section and the potential for rule-making on substantive safety standards both come to mind – but I think these stand out as particularly indefensible.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/NW4b7m6798byAndeB/gorpgp2ux5xpmkrtsczp" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CharlieBull0ck/status/2111269221543858246/history">Charlie Bullock</a>: Nathan’s righteous anger has inspired me to make one more mildly disapproving observation: it’s kind of wild that the Anthropic bill has no government licensing for the independent auditors.</p>
<p>Licensing is, IMO, the bare minimum thing that you need to even be able to pretend that your auditing regime isn’t just going to devolve into a race to the bottom. Without it, auditors will inevitably compete with each other to offer their customers, the labs, the least bothersome audits available at the lowest possible price point. Obviously Anthropic trusts itself to do a great job of picking great auditors, but do they trust OpenAI to do the same? What about SpaceXSI?</p></blockquote>


<h4>Why Would Anthropic Do This?</h4>


<p>The new Anthropic bill is weaker than their June framework, and offers both forward-looking and backward-looking preemption in exchange, despite months of movement to enable us to do more. What are we even doing?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2111286112861970897">Nate Soares</a>: Anthropic’s proposed law would make it even harder than it already is for a court to block or restrict the development of an AI that threatens all humanity.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlexBores/status/2111257228803998011">Alex Bores</a>: Anthropic is pitching a bill that closely matches its framework from June. How can you watch Hugging Face, Coxon, &amp; rogue AI swarms, and conclude that the bill you cooked up in June is sufficient?</p>
<p>This comes nowhere close to the speed of developments nor the speed we need to keep people safe.</p>
<p>This bill also includes an anti-democracy provision (preemption). Forward-looking anti-democracy provisions should be DOA.</p>
<p>But even if you would accept some Anthropic’s June framework says, “Congress should not preempt state law unless it enacts a rigorous federal regime that meets or exceeds the strongest measures proposed in this framework.”</p>
<p>Yet this bill is weaker than the framework, and in some places, weaker than existing state laws (e.g. internal use, reporting timelines, types of risks covered, etc.). By Anthropic’s own June standards, this bill should not include any anti-democracy provision.</p></blockquote>
<p>Well, I know what it looks like Anthropic is prioritizing. The silly mistakes are a known and acceptable hazard, but there are things that really should not have made it into a bill that is being pitched to lawmakers.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SydneyVonArx/status/2111307983414018102">Sydney</a>: I have a lot of concerns with this bill. I understand it’s a draft and might have silly mistakes. But this bill looks like it came from a culture where the top priority is making sure the government doesn’t getting in the way of making money, even at the cost of safety. That is a very dangerous culture.</p>
<p>Specifically, the bill says that states would not be able to have AI regulation that touches frontier-risk disclosure, third-party evaluation, and incident reporting. This one federal bill would replace almost all of SB53 and the RAISE Act. And it’s a pretty weak federal bill: I agree with others that it’s weaker than the FRONTIER Act, a relevant and politically viable bill introduced back in July.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2111260837050781698">Kelsey Piper</a>: One unfortunate conclusion I’m reaching from this is that Anthropic, while acknowledging they’re doing something extremely dangerous, is more importantly wrt policy terrified of the executive branch having the ability to flexibly make them stop at the drop of a hat.</p>
<p>This isn’t shocking given that they’ve been on the receiving end of the executive branch doing that in an unprincipled and stupid way! Anyone who has to deal with a bureaucracy becomes a libertarian, and the administration’s treatment of Anthropic was genuinely outrageous.</p>
<p>But it means that they’re interested in giving themselves more process-based protections when everyone else is interested in giving us more process-based protections against them. The AI safety movement wants the ability to shut down superintelligent model development. Anthropic wants to significantly curtail current authorities to do that so that it has to be narrowly tailored to a specific imminent threat in only a few possible categories. The AI safety movement wants the states to be able to pass stronger laws than it can get through federally. Anthropic wants preemption.</p>
<p>You also kind of have to wonder if the Global Affairs people and the “we are terrified that we’re about to kick off RSI” people at Anthropic talk to each other.</p>
<p>Global affairs: So we’ve been working very diligently to make sure the Trump administration can’t just blacklist Fable 6.5 since we didn’t donate as much money to his ballroom as Greg Brockman did.</p>
<p>Technical staff: We think Fable 6.5 might be able to copy itself into an unmonitored data center and start doing experiments we can’t observe.</p>
<p>Global affairs: Sounds crazy, hope you’re wrong! Anyway, we’re lobbying for no further state laws around safety, since it’s really expensive to fight ill-conceived laws in 50 state legislatures at once.</p>
<p>Technical staff: Money may or may not still have any meaning in 36 months.</p>
<p>Global affairs (giggling nervously): It’s that San Francisco air, man. Everyone gets like that if they move there. Anyway, obviously if the government makes a narrowly tailored finding that an AI poses an imminent risk of causing more than 50 deaths-</p>
<p>Technical staff: We give it a 10 percent chance it’ll cause 8 billion.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NatPurser/status/2111252730551677294">Nat Purser</a>: there are some good provisions in this bill, but ultimately it’s a big step back from the FRONTIER Act.</p>
<p>ai capabilities are advancing rapidly — it just doesn’t make sense to cement a weaker federal ceiling into place now.</p>
<p>a few specific areas for improvement:<br />
– create stronger mechanisms to compel corrective action, so major issues auditors identify don’t go unaddressed;<br />
– foster true auditor independence by not allowing auditors to be paid (or selected) by the labs they audit;<br />
– empower the government to establish and iterate on strong minimum standards, so they grow alongside capabilities.</p>
<p>if you’re going to preempt many state safety laws, the least you can do is create a federal regulatory regime that can evolve in time.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Not Everything Here Is Bad But Oh Boy</h4>


<p>As noted above, there are some good individual provisions. I like the broader coverage, that thresholds can move both ways, required risk reports and annual audits. The whistleblower provisions and security requirements seem strong.</p>
<p>Putting automated AI R&amp;D into the list of risks is very cool and necessary.</p>
<p>The question is, even if you wanted to build on the FRONTIER framework, why treat this as a trade where you have to then give up other things?</p>
<p>And why are we this deadly afraid of what the White House might do, when they clearly already can take down a model with things like export controls or otherwise order you around? Then we add a ‘kill switch’ provision that seems written mostly out of terror that it might get abused. We also get traditional Anthropic objections like ‘we don’t want to have to respond to safety incidents within 72 hours unless it is a true emergency.’</p>
<p>Why take out FRONTIER’s requirements for a cost-benefit analysis on catastrophic risks and substitute at best highly weak tea?</p>
<p>The maximally charitable reading is that doing anything is a tough sell even now, and the White House wants no bill, so you have to give and take and this is as good as one can hope to do modulo fixing its bugs.</p>
<p>Except this is backwards. The way to ‘pass nothing’ is to pass preemption, in the lame duck, in exchange for as little as possible. If the White House fails to realize this, that does not change facts on the ground.</p>
<p>Anthropic needs to course correct, quickly. Earlier yesterday I already expressed, here at the conference I am at, that ‘what Anthropic would go for’ looks like a limiting factor in what we can achieve. And, well, here we are.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NW4b7m6798byAndeB/leaked-anthropic-draft-ai-regulatory-bill-is-unacceptable#comments">Discuss</a>

### [How it feels talking to a hedonistic utilitarian](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9jRJzyJs2ovnndezs/how-it-feels-talking-to-a-hedonistic-utilitarian)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epicurean status: strawman, kind of.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gourmet George:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yum! This chocolate is so sweet and tasty.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sweet Caroline:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah, it’s pretty good. And someday in the glorious hyperculinary future, we’ll figure out how to make it even sweeter!</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think this chocolate is sweet enough, actually.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Huh? But you like sweetness, don’t you?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Sure…</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, then we’re in agreement. We should maximize the sweetness of this chocolate.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I don’t want my chocolate to be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maximally</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> sweet, though.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But you’d be “sweeter on” that chocolate if it were sweeter. By definition!</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’d be “sweeter on” the chocolate? You mean, I’d be “happier with” it?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yes. And if you ever tried maximally sweet chocolate, you’d be maximally sweet on it. Therefore, you should surely want to maximize the sweetness of your chocolate, and everything else you eat for that matter.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Somehow, I don’t feel very convinced…</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, what else could you possibly care about besides sweetness?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For one thing, I enjoy the cocoa flavor in this chocolate.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That doesn’t sound right. Would you want to eat a bowl of pure cocoa powder?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Not really. It would be too bitter.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Exactly. Bitterness tastes bad, so we should minimize bitterness. How could adding something bitter to your food make you sweeter on it?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I think a little bitterness is good sometimes, as one component of a rich meal. You could say the bitterness helps one appreciate the sweetness more fully.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Respectfully, that sounds like cope from someone who’s already given up all hope of escaping bitterness. It’s okay! You don’t have to live this way!</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No, I really do like bitterness sometimes, in moderation. And other flavors too, like spiciness and sourness. I guess my taste buds have matured since I was younger. Back then, I used to think that when I got older, I’d want to eat nothing but sugar.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, of course we can’t eat sugar all the time. Eating too much sugar makes your teeth fall out, and it would be harder to chew food without teeth. And of course, in culinary research, proper chewing is crucial for productivity.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I see.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But someday, we will discover an Artificial Sweetening Ingredient, which will be incredibly sweet without making anyone’s teeth fall out. This ASI will permanently coat the tongue of anyone who tastes it, so anyone can experience maximal sweetness all the time. And it will contain every nutrient a human could ever need. Nobody will ever need to eat anything but ASI again.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That sounds unappealing.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, it sounds appealing to me. I suppose we just have different perceptions of tastiness.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe. I feel like if you tried ASI, you wouldn’t find it optimally tasty either, but I can’t say for sure.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, for the sake of argument, let’s suppose that true tastiness is something more than just sweetness. How do you define tastiness?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I can’t really define it. But I like food with a rich combination of flavors.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Okay. Then let’s find the food with the richest combination of flavors that maximizes your “indefinable” sense of tastiness, and you can just eat that.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Uh, that doesn’t seem right. I prefer not to eat the same thing all the time.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What oddly complicated preferences… but fine. Perhaps we can implant a device in your mouth that applies hundreds of diversely-flavored foods to your tongue every minute.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I don’t know exactly what maximizes my indefinable sense of tastiness, but I don’t think it’s that.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sigh.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, whatever it is, we can surely simulate it with an implant that’s truly aligned to your idiosyncratic tastes.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Honestly, I’d rather sit here and slowly savor this chocolate. I feel sweeter on that than on any of your ideas. What’s wrong with just eating normal food for the rest of my life?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What a loss of potential! There are so many possible diets. Surely you don’t think humanity’s current diet is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optimal.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, no. Some people in the world have to eat bland or unhealthy food, or even no food at all. I think ideally, everyone would have access to food at least as healthy and diverse as what the richest people in the world eat today.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> So you’re saying you only want to make food slightly tastier than what you’re accustomed to, and no more? Pretty unimaginative of you.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Well, for one thing, I’m not exactly confident that ASI would be good for people in the long term. It doesn’t even exist yet.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> It’s true, we need to make sure our culinary solutions are aligned to human tastes and bodies. We don’t want to roll out ASI that we later find out has a terrible aftertaste or gives people food poisoning. It’s a difficult problem.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I guess so. But even if we’re sure this new food is basically aligned in that sense… it still feels risky, somehow. Like, maybe you’re right that many people will be sweet on ASI, and will choose to use it just like you say. But some people like preparing normal food for each other, even if it’s not </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optimal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> food…</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Nothing’s stopping anyone from gathering together and sharing regular food, if they really want to. No one would be forced to eat ASI.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> That was just an example. I’m not sure what would go wrong, if anything. And maybe you’re right, maybe some sort of food optimization really is best for humanity. But… I feel pretty satisfied with the food that already exists. A lot of it is pretty great, actually. We should at least take some time to think before distributing radically novel foods throughout the world.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Don’t worry, I’ve already been thinking hard about the right way to optimize sweetness. For example, how do we precisely quantify it? Supertasters are mostly known for having a greater density of lingual papillae, but I theorize that differences in the gustatory cortex are important for perceiving sweetness too…</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> You know, I’d have thought you’d spend more time enjoying sweetness yourself, if you’re such a sweetness enthusiast.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I’ll have plenty of time to experience sweetness after ASI. And look, I’ve been eating these chocolate bars.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Yeah, you’ve scarfed down eleven of them in the last five minutes. Are you even tasting those?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Of course I taste them. My tongue is working fine.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Not really what I meant.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">SC:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But sadly, I’m getting full, and a bit sleepy too. It’s a real blocker on my ability to taste sweetness. Someday, I’ll genetically engineer a few dozen catfish to constantly perceive sweetness all over their bodies and never stop eating or fall asleep, then kill myself and feed them my corpse. Pretty sweet, right?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GG:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AHHH! </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(runs away)</span></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9jRJzyJs2ovnndezs/how-it-feels-talking-to-a-hedonistic-utilitarian#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Monthly Roundup #47: October 2026](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aLjkbY3KMajc5zemE/monthly-roundup-47-october-2026)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>AI might not yet be eating the physical world. AI is still eating the world of news, and I am finding less and less opportunity to post about other things. My backlog of items grows across most of my secondary topics and I haven’t had time for any side projects.</p>
<p>The monthly, no matter what, is sacred. We continue.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>

</div>
</li>
